Eigenvector fluctuations and limit results for random graphs with infinite rank kernels
Minh Tang, Joshua Cape

Abstract
This paper systematically studies the behavior of the leading eigenvectors for independent edge undirected random graphs generated from a general latent position model whose link function is possibly infinite rank and also possibly indefinite. We first derive uniform error bounds in the two-to-infinity norm as well as row-wise normal approximations for the leading sample eigenvectors. We then build on these results to tackle two graph inference problems, namely (i) entrywise bounds for graphon estimation and (ii) testing for the equality of latent positions, the latter of which is achieved by proposing a rank-adaptive test statistic that converges in distribution to a weighted sum of independent chi-square random variables under the null hypothesis. Our fine-grained theoretical guarantees and applications differ from the existing literature which primarily considers first order upper bounds and more restrictive low rank or positive semidefinite model assumptions. Further, our results collectively quantify the statistical properties of eigenvector-based spectral embeddings with growing dimensionality for large graphs.



1
Introduction

Statistical network analysis, encompassing the study of statistical methodology, theory, and applications for networks or graph data, has witnessed a surge of attention and progress in recent decades. Driven in large part by developments in data collection capabilities and pressing scientific questions, the overarching objective of this domain is to develop the statistical foundations of network or graph data analysis. A principal challenge here is that such data are complex, inter-related, structured, and hence fall outside the scope of traditional data settings in classical Statistics.

Historically, considerable attention has been devoted to exploratory graph analysis, network modeling, and problems of estimation, areas which continue to witness ongoing developments. More recently, building on these achievements, there has been increasing interest in developing genuine inference procedures that quantify uncertainty in order to address network hypothesis testing problems for popular random graph models.

This paper undertakes a detailed study of spectral methods and their statistical inference capabilities for analyzing large, independent edge, undirected, inhomogeneous random graphs generated from a general latent position network model. Given a symmetric adjacency matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
generated from an underlying symmetric edge probability matrix
P\in[0,1]^{n\times n}
determined by a kernel
\kappa
(of possibly infinite rank), we study
r
-dimensional spectral embeddings of the form
\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{\alpha}
, where
\alpha\in\{0,1/2,1\}
, where
\widehat{\Lambda}
is the
r\times r
diagonal matrix of largest-in-magnitude eigenvalues of
A
, and where
\widehat{U}
is an
n\times r
matrix containing corresponding orthonormal eigenvectors of
A
. We show that
\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{\alpha}
is entrywise and row-wise close (modulo a necessary orthogonal transformation
W
) to the corresponding population-level spectral embedding
U|\Lambda|^{\alpha}
, determined by
P
, in a wide variety of settings (see
Theorems

1
,
1
,
2
and
3
). For
\alpha=1/2
, we establish row-wise multivariate asymptotic normality in
Corollary

2
, with similar results holding for
\alpha=0,1
. Our results are enabled by new deterministic matrix perturbation analysis (see
Theorem

5
) and careful treatment of concentration inequalities including novel leave-one-out analysis (see
Appendix

A
).

Numerous estimation and inference problems for both single and multiple networks can be formulated and tackled via the spectral embeddings given by
\alpha\in\{0,1/2,1\}
. Examples include community detection via spectral clustering, vertex nomination, two-sample hypothesis testing, and more; see
Section

4
for additional discussion and references. In this paper,
Section

4.1
demonstrates how our results give entrywise bounds on both edge probability matrix estimation and graphon estimation.
Section

4.2
considers the problem of testing for the equality of latent positions, for which we propose a flexible plug-in test statistic that adapts to the embedding dimension (rank truncation value)
r
, even when
r\equiv r(n)\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
(see
Theorems

4
and
4
).
Section

5
provides simulation examples illustrating the finite-sample properties of our proposed test procedure.

Our results are exceedingly general and hence broadly applicable, in the following aspects.

1.

We obtain results both for low (i.e., finite) rank models and for infinite rank models.

2.

We obtain results both for models with positive semidefinite kernels and for more general (i.e., possibly indefinite) kernels.

3.

We obtain results without requiring unnecessary or purely mathematically convenient assumptions such as population-level bounded coherence (i.e., eigenvector delocalization).

4.

We obtain results both for settings with and without repeated population eigenvalues.

5.

Our results hold for eigenvector-based embeddings with fixed or growing dimensionality for large networks.

By addressing both the deviations and fluctuations of spectral embeddings derived from the adjacency-based (scaled) sample eigenvectors, this paper simultaneously recovers and improves upon numerous existing results in the literature in a unifying fashion
[
1
,
4
,
15
,
17
,
25
,
27
,
39
,
38
,
61
]
.

This paper is situated in the concrete setting of random graphs and demonstrates a wide range of results that can be obtained therein. At the same time, we emphasize that the considerations in this paper can, if desired, be extended beyond networks to signal-plus-noise type models more generally and other data settings, following for example the blueprint in
[
16
]
and elsewhere
[
60
]
. While conceptually straightforward in principle (see
Theorem

5
), doing so would require the interested researcher to adapt and customize current technical lemmas and proof arguments (see
Appendix

B
) to their specified data setting of interest.

1.1
Notations

We summarize some notations frequently used in this paper. For a positive integer
p
, let
[p]
denote the set
\{1,\dots,p\}
. For two non-negative sequences
\{a_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\{b_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
, we write
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
(
a_{n}\gtrsim b_{n}
, resp.) if there exists some constant
C>0
such that
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
(
a_{n}\geq Cb_{n}
, resp.) for all
n\geq 1
. We write
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
if simultaneously
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
and
a_{n}\gtrsim b_{n}
. If
a_{n}/b_{n}
stays bounded away from
+\infty
, then we write
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
and
b_{n}=\Omega(a_{n})
. We write
a_{n}=\Theta(b_{n})
to indicate that
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
and
a_{n}=\Omega(b_{n})
. If
a_{n}/b_{n}\to 0
, then we write
a_{n}=o(b_{n})
and
b_{n}=\omega(a_{n})
.

We say that a sequence of events
\{\mathcal{A}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
holds with high probability if for any constant
c>0
there exists a finite constant
n_{0}
depending only on
c
such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{n})\geq 1-n^{-c}
for all
n\geq n_{0}
.

In this paper, all vectors and matrices are real-valued. We let
\mathcal{O}_{d}
denote the set of
d\times d
orthogonal matrices. Given a matrix
M
, we denote its spectral norm by
\|M\|
, its Frobenius norm by
\|M\|_{F}
, its maximum absolute row sum norm by
\|M\|_{\infty}
, its nuclear norm by
\|M\|_{\ast}
, and its maximum absolute entrywise norm by
\|M\|_{\max}
.

We denote the two-to-infinity norm (
2\to\infty
norm) of the matrix
M
by

\|M\|_{2\to\infty}=\max_{\|x\|=1}\|Mx\|_{\infty}\equiv\max_{i}\|m_{i}\|,

where
\|x\|
denotes the Euclidean norm of the vector
x
, and
m_{i}
denotes the
i
-th row vector of
M
. In particular,
\|M\|_{2\to\infty}
is the maximum row-wise
\ell_{2}
norm of
M
. We emphasize that the
2\to\infty
norm is not sub-multiplicative in general, though, for any matrices
M
and
N
of conformable dimensions, it holds that (e.g., see
[
15
, Proposition 6.5]
)

\|MN\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\min\left\{\|M\|_{2\to\infty}\times\|N\|,\|M\|_{\infty}\times\|N\|_{2\to\infty}\right\}.

(1.1)

Perturbation bounds using the
2\to\infty
norm for the eigenvectors or singular vectors of a noisily observed matrix have recently attracted widespread interest in the statistics community. For example, see
[
20
,
15
,
2
,
26
,
1
,
62
,
19
,
12
,
21
]
and the numerous references therein.

2
Setup

This paper considers latent position random graphs
[
30
,
9
]
specified as follows.

Definition 1

(Latent position graph)
.

Given a positive integer
d\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a nonempty compact set, and let
F
be a probability distribution taking values in
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\kappa:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[0,1]
be a symmetric measurable function, namely
\kappa(x,x^{\prime})=\kappa(x^{\prime},x)
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
. We say that
(A,X)
is a
latent position graph
on
n
vertices with distribution
F
, link function
\kappa
, and sparsity
\rho_{n}
when
A
is generated via the following procedure.

1.

Sample i.i.d. latent positions
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}
according to
F
.

2.

Define
P
as the
n\times n
matrix with entries
P_{ij}=\rho_{n}\kappa(X_{i},X_{j})
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

3.

Given
P
, sample a symmetric binary matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
whose upper-triangular entries
A_{ij}
are independent Bernoulli random variables with
\mathbb{P}(A_{ij}=1)=P_{ij}
for
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
.

For simplicity, we indicate this setting by writing
A\sim\mathrm{LPG}(\kappa,F;\rho_{n})
.

Remark 1

(Latent position graph versus graphon)
.

If
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]
and
F
is associated with Lebesgue measure, then
\kappa
in
Definition

1
is widely known as a
graphon

[
40
]
. While any latent position graph distribution is equivalent (with respect to the cut metric) to some graphon
[
10
, Proposition 10]
and vice versa, there are benefits to considering these models separately, due to the fact that additional structure is possible in higher dimensions. For example, suppose that
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]^{d}
for
d\geq 2
is equipped with Lebesgue measure and that
\kappa
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous. Then, any graphon
\widetilde{\kappa}
on
[0,1]\times[0,1]
equivalent to
\kappa
may only be
(\alpha/d)
-Hölder continuous
[
34
]
.
∎

For ease of exposition, we shall first assume that
\kappa
is a continuous,
positive semidefinite
kernel. Results for general (indefinite) kernels can be obtained similarly but are deferred to
Section

3.1
because they require substantially more involved notation and derivations.

Concretely,
\kappa
being continuous and positive semidefinite implies the following useful facts.

Proposition 1

(Properties of positive semidefinite kernels)
.

Let
\kappa
in
Definition

1
be a continuous, positive semidefinite kernel, and let
\mathscr{K}
denote the integral operator induced by
\kappa
, namely

(\mathscr{K}h)(x)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\kappa(x,y)h(y)\,\mathrm{d}F(y)

(2.1)

for any square-integrable real-valued function
h\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
. Let
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0

be the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
, and let
\{\phi_{r}\}_{r\geq 1}
be the corresponding orthonormal eigenfunctions satisfying

\int_{\mathcal{X}}\phi_{r}(x)\phi_{s}(x)\mathrm{d}F(x)=\begin{cases}1&\text{~if~~}r=s,\\
0&\text{~if~~}r\neq s,\end{cases}

for all integers
r,s\geq 1
.

1.

For any integer
r\geq 1
, let
U
be the
n\times r
matrix whose columns are orthonormal eigenvectors corresponding to the
r
largest-in-magnitude eigenvalues of
P
. Let
\Lambda
be the diagonal matrix containing the corresponding eigenvalues of
P
. Then, with probability one,

\|U|\Lambda|^{1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}=\|U|\Lambda|U^{\top}\|_{\max}^{1/2}\leq\|P\|_{\max}^{1/2}\leq\rho_{n}^{1/2}.

(2.2)

Note that the above bound does not depend on
r
.
Similarly,
\|U_{\perp}|\Lambda_{\perp}|^{1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\rho_{n}^{1/2}
holds with probability one.

2.

By Mercer’s theorem (e.g., see
[
51
, Theorem 4.49]
) it holds that

\kappa(x,y)=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\mu_{r}\phi_{r}(x)\phi_{r}(y),

(2.3)

where the sum converges absolutely and uniformly on
\mathrm{supp}(F)\times\mathrm{supp}(F)
.

3.

Furthermore,
\mathscr{K}
is a compact, positive operator and of trace-class, namely
\mathscr{K}=|\mathscr{K}|
, where
|\mathscr{K}|=\sqrt{\mathscr{K}^{\operatorname{H}}\mathscr{K}}
denotes the positive semidefinite Hermitian square root, and

\mathrm{tr}\left(|\mathscr{K}|\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mu_{j}=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\kappa(x,x)\mathrm{d}F(x)<\infty.

(2.4)

The matrix
P
, properly normalized, is itself positive and of trace-class. Namely, with probability one,

\frac{1}{n\rho_{n}}\,\mathrm{tr}\left(|P|\right)=\frac{1}{n\rho_{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}=\frac{1}{n\rho_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{n}\kappa(X_{i},X_{i})\leq 1.

4.

The eigenvalues of
P
, written
\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{n}
, are consistent for those of
\mathscr{K}
. In particular, for any
c>0
, by
[
47
, Theorem 7]
it holds that

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{j\geq 1}\left|(n\rho_{n})^{-1}\lambda_{j}-\mu_{j}\right|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2c}\log^{1/2}{n}}{n^{1/2}}\right)\geq 1-2n^{-c},

(2.5)

where, by definition,
\lambda_{j}=0
whenever
j>n
.

We note that
Eq.

2.2
and
Eq.

2.5
also hold when
\kappa
is discontinuous but positive semidefinite. The uniform convergence in
Eq.

2.3
and the trace formula in
Eq.

2.4
, however, do require continuity of
\kappa
.

Remark 2

(Population eigenvectors and coherence)
.

Eqs.

2.2
and
1.1
together imply that
\|U\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\rho_{n}^{1/2}\lambda_{r}^{-1/2}
holds with probability one. Define
\mathfrak{c}(U)=\tfrac{n}{r}\|U\|_{2\to\infty}^{2}
be the
coherence
of
U
(see
[
14
, Definition 1.2]
). Now, if we choose
r
such that
\mu_{r}\geq 4\sqrt{2c}n^{-1/2}\log^{1/2}{n}
then
\lambda_{r}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}n\rho_{n}\mu_{r}
holds with probability at least
1-2n^{-c}
(see
Eq.

2.5
), and hence
\mathfrak{c}(U)\leq 2(r\mu_{r})^{-1}
holds with probability at least
1-2n^{-c}
. If we fix
r
, then the previous bound indicates that
U
has
bounded coherence
with high probability. However, in this paper, we are interested in letting
r
grow with
n
, in which case
\mathfrak{c}(U)
can diverge with
n
since
\mu_{r}
typically decays to zero at a faster rate than
r^{-1}
. See
Remarks

5
and
6
for additional discussion and specifics.
∎

3
Main results

We begin by presenting our first main result for (scaled) eigenvector estimation in latent position graphs with positive semidefinite kernels.

Theorem 1

(Fine-grained eigenvector perturbation analysis for LPGs with positive semidefinite kernels)
.

Let
A\sim\mathrm{LPG}(\kappa,F;\rho_{n})
be a graph on
n
vertices generated according to
Definition

1
, where
\kappa
is positive semidefinite. For a given
r\geq 1
, let
U
and
\widehat{U}
be the
n\times r
matrices whose orthonormal columns are the leading eigenvectors of
A
and
P
, respectively, and let the diagonal matrices
\widehat{\Lambda}
and
\Lambda
contain the corresponding eigenvalues of
A
and
P
. Fix
\nu>0
and suppose
r\geq 1
is chosen such that the conditions

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}\geq\max\left\{4\varsigma(\nu,n),\tfrac{16}{3}(\nu+2)\log n\right\},

(3.1)

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}\geq\max\left\{16(\nu+2)\log n+\frac{64\varsigma(\nu,n)^{2}}{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}},\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)\right\},

(3.2)

are both satisfied, where

\displaystyle\varsigma(\nu,n)=2\sqrt{2en\rho_{n}}+(56\sqrt{e}+\sqrt{2\nu})\sqrt{\log n},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\vartheta(c,r,n)=c\log n+r\log 9,\qquad\text{for $c>0$}.

(3.4)

Let
\delta_{r}=\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}
.
Then, there exists an
r\times r
orthogonal matrix
W^{(n)}
such that

\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}^{1/2}W^{(n)}-U\Lambda^{1/2}=EU\Lambda^{-1/2}+Q,

(3.5)

where
EU\Lambda^{-1/2}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|EU\Lambda^{-1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\frac{11}{2}\rho_{n}^{1/2}\lambda_{r}^{-1/2}\sqrt{\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)}

(3.6)

with probability at least
1-n^{-\nu}
, while
Q
satisfies

\displaystyle\|Q\|_{2\to\infty}\lesssim\frac{n\rho_{n}^{3/2}}{\delta_{r}^{2}}+\frac{(\rho_{n}\log n)^{1/2}}{\lambda_{r}^{1/2}}\left(\frac{(rn\rho_{n})^{1/2}+\log n}{\delta_{r}}\right)

(3.7)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-\nu})
. Above, the notation
\lesssim
and
O(\cdot)
hides universal constants that depend only on
\nu
but not on
r
,
n
,
\rho_{n}
, or
\kappa
.

As
n\rho_{n}\geq\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{r}\geq\delta_{r}
,

Eq.

3.1
through
Eq.

3.4
automatically imply a lower bound of
n\rho_{n}=\Omega(\log n)
as typically seen in the literature on spectral inference for random graphs.
Furthermore, it holds that
\|Q\|_{2\to\infty}=o(\lambda_{r}^{-1/2}\rho_{n}^{1/2}(r^{1/2}+\log^{1/2}n))
with high probability, so
EU\Lambda^{-1/2}
is the dominant term in the expansion of
Eq.

3.5
, whenever the conditions

\displaystyle\delta_{r}^{4}(r+\log n)=\omega\left((n\rho_{n})^{2}\lambda_{r}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\delta_{r}=\omega\left(\max\left\{\sqrt{n\rho_{n}\log n},\log n\right\}\right)

(3.8)

are simultaneously satisfied.

Remark 3

(Explicit constants and bounding the residual perturbation term)
.

Derivations of explicit but not-too-large universal constants in
Eq.

3.1
through
Eq.

3.4
are crucial for finite-sample inference as they allow us to choose
r
, interpretable as the embedding dimension or dimensionality, as growing with
n
in a data-dependent manner (see
Corollary

1
below) while
simultaneously
guaranteeing that the bound in
Eq.

3.6
for the main order term holds for any finite
n
.
Meanwhile, explicit albeit possibly sub-optimal values for the constants appearing in the bound for the residual term,
\|Q\|_{2\to\infty}
, can be derived through careful but tedious book-keeping per the technical lemmas in
Appendix

B
. For example, one can show

\begin{split}\|Q\|_{2\to\infty}&\leq\frac{74\rho_{n}^{1/2}\varsigma(\nu,n)^{2}}{\delta_{r}^{2}}+\frac{181\rho_{n}\sqrt{\vartheta(\nu+2,r,n)}}{\delta_{r}}+\frac{80\varsigma(\nu,n)\sqrt{(\nu+2)r\rho_{n}\log n}}{\delta_{r}\lambda_{r}^{1/2}}\\
&\qquad+\frac{\sqrt{\rho_{n}\vartheta(\nu+2,r,n)}}{\lambda_{r}^{1/2}}\left(\frac{176(\nu+2)\log n+357\varsigma(\nu,n)}{\delta_{r}}\right)\end{split}

holds with probability at least
1-10n^{-\nu}
. See
Section

A.1
for more details.
∎

Remark 4

(On dimension selection and eigengaps in high rank models)
.

This paper is primarily concerned with the setting
\mathrm{rk}(\kappa)=\infty
, where
\mathrm{rk}(\kappa)
denotes the number of non-zero eigenvalues associated with the integral operator
\mathscr{K}
in
Eq.

2.1
. Notably, if
\mathrm{rk}(\kappa)=\infty
, then for any
n\geq 1
, the
n\times n
matrix
P
in
Definition

1
is of rank
n
with probability one. In this setting, it is often desirable to choose the embedding dimension,
r
, to change (i.e., grow) with
n
, such as in the inference problems considered in
Sections

4.1
and
4.2
. Importantly, doing so is always possible when
A
is generated according to the model in
Definition

1
with
n\rho_{n}=\omega(\log n)
. More specifically, for any fixed but arbitrary
s\geq 1
, there always exists
r\geq s
for which
Eqs.

3.1
and
3.2
are satisfied as
n
increases. Indeed, as
\mathscr{K}
is compact, the only accumulation point of
\{\mu_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
is at
0
, hence if
\mu_{s}>0
, then there always exists some
r\geq s
such that
\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}>0
. For this choice of
r
, by
Eq.

2.5
, we have for any
c>0
and sufficiently large
n
that

\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}\geq n\rho_{n}(\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}-4\sqrt{2c}n^{-1/2}\log^{1/2}{n})\geq\tfrac{1}{2}n\rho_{n}(\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1})\geq 4\varsigma(\nu,n)

with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
, and
Eq.

3.1
is satisfied. Given
Eq.

3.1
, for any fixed
c
,
\nu
, and
r
, we also have

\begin{split}\lambda_{r}&\geq n\rho_{n}(\mu_{r}-2\sqrt{2c}n^{-1/2}\log^{1/2}{n})\\
&\geq\max\left\{16(\nu+2)\log n+\frac{64\varsigma(\nu,n)^{2}}{\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1}},\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)\right\}\end{split}

for sufficiently large
n
, and
Eq.

3.2
is also satisfied. In summary, if
\mathrm{rk}(\kappa)=\infty
, then we can always select a sequence
\{r(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
, with
r(n)\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
for which
Theorem

1
applies. Furthermore, for any
r
along this sequence, the bounds for
EU\Lambda^{-1/2}
and
Q
can be written in terms of
\{\mu_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
as

\displaystyle\|EU\Lambda^{-1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\lesssim\frac{(r^{1/2}+\log^{1/2}{n})}{n^{1/2}\mu_{r}^{1/2}},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\|Q\|_{2\to\infty}\lesssim\frac{1}{n\rho_{n}^{1/2}(\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1})^{2}}+\frac{\log^{1/2}{n}}{n\rho_{n}^{1/2}\mu_{r}^{1/2}(\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1})}\left(r^{1/2}+\frac{\log n}{(n\rho_{n})^{1/2}}\right),

(3.10)

which hold with high probability.
∎

For ease of exposition, the conditions for
r
in
Theorem

1
are stated in terms of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{r}
and
\lambda_{r+1}
of the edge probability matrix
P
. As
P
is unknown, the next result,
Corollary

1
, replaces these conditions with those based on the eigenvalues
\widehat{\lambda}_{r}
and
\widehat{\lambda}_{r+1}
of
A
. For simplicity,
\rho_{n}
is assumed to be known, though if
\rho_{n}
is unknown then we can replace
n\rho_{n}
with
\Delta\log^{1/2}{n}
in
Eq.

3.12
, where
\Delta
is the average degree of
A
. In particular,
\Delta\log^{1/2}{n}=\omega(n\rho_{n})
holds asymptotically almost surely. Furthermore, since
\max_{i\geq 1}|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-\lambda_{i}|\leq\|E\|
holds by Weyl’s inequality, applying
Lemma

4
yields
\|E\|\leq\varsigma(\nu,n)
with probability at least
1-n^{-\nu}
, which yields the conditions for
r
in
Eqs.

3.11
and
3.12
.

Corollary 1

(Data-driven dimension selection in
Theorem

1
)
.

Assume the setting and notations in
Theorem

1
.
Suppose
r\geq 1
is chosen such that the conditions

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}_{r}-\widehat{\lambda}_{r+1}\geq\max\left\{4\varsigma(\nu,n),\tfrac{16}{3}(\nu+2)\log n\right\}+2\varsigma(\nu,n),

(3.11)

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}_{r}\geq\max\left\{16(\nu+2)\log n+\frac{64\varsigma(\nu,n)^{2}}{\widehat{\lambda}_{r}-\widehat{\lambda}_{r+1}},\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)\right\}+\varsigma(\nu,n),

(3.12)

are both satisfied, where
\varsigma
and
\vartheta
are defined in
Eqs.

3.3
and
3.4
.
Then,
Eq.

3.5
holds,
where
EU\Lambda^{-1/2}
and
Q
therein satisfy the bounds in
Eqs.

3.6
and
3.7
with probability at least
1-O(n^{-\nu})
.

Remark 5

(Eigenvalues with polynomial decay)
.

Suppose that the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
exhibit polynomial decay, i.e.,
\mu_{r}\asymp r^{-\alpha}
and
\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}\asymp r^{-\beta}
for some constant
\beta>\alpha>1
. In this setting,

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}\asymp n\rho_{n}r^{-\alpha},\quad\text{and}\quad\delta_{r}\asymp n\rho_{n}r^{-\beta},

\displaystyle\|EU\Lambda^{-1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\lesssim\frac{r^{(1+\alpha)/2}\log^{1/2}{n}}{n^{1/2}},

\displaystyle\|Q\|_{2\to\infty}\lesssim\frac{r^{2\beta}+r^{\alpha+\beta}\log^{1/2}{n}}{n\rho_{n}^{1/2}}+\frac{r^{(1+\alpha)/2+\beta}\log^{3/2}{n}}{n^{3/2}\rho_{n}}.

If
r
is chosen such that

r\ll\min\left\{(n\rho_{n})^{\tfrac{1}{\alpha+2\beta-1}},(n\rho_{n}\log n)^{\tfrac{1}{4\beta-\alpha-1}},(n\rho_{n}\log^{-1}{n})^{\tfrac{1}{\beta}}\right\},

(3.13)

then
\|Q\|_{2\to\infty}
is negligible compared to
\|EU\Lambda^{-1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}
. If instead,
r
is chosen such that

r\ll\min\left\{(n\rho_{n}\log^{-1}{n})^{\tfrac{3}{1+\alpha+2\beta}},(n\rho_{n}\log^{-1/2}{n})^{\tfrac{1}{\alpha+\beta}},(n\rho_{n})^{\tfrac{1}{2\beta}}\right\},

then
\|EU\Lambda^{-1/2}+Q\|_{2\to\infty}=o(\rho_{n}^{1/2})
with high probability. Ignoring logarithmic factors in
n
, the above condition corresponds to
r^{2\beta}=o(n\rho_{n})
which is slightly more restrictive than the condition
\lambda_{r}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\rho_{n}})
in the matrix USVT literature
[
61
,
17
]
. Indeed, for the current setting,
\lambda_{r}=\Omega(\sqrt{n\rho_{n}})
is equivalent to
r^{2\alpha}=O(n\rho_{n})
. Given that subspace estimation is generally more difficult than low-rank approximation (e.g., see
[
24
]
), we conjecture that the condition
r^{2\beta}=o(n\rho_{n})
is not easily improvable.
∎

Remark 6

(Eigenvalues with exponential decay)
.

Suppose now that the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
exhibit exponential decay, i.e.,
\mu_{r}\asymp\exp(-c_{0}r^{\beta})
and
\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}\asymp r^{\alpha}\exp(-c_{0}r^{\beta})
for some constants
c_{0}>0
,
\alpha\geq{0}
, and
\beta\in(0,1]
. This assumption on
\mu_{r}
arises naturally whenever the kernel
\kappa
is sufficiently smooth (e.g., infinitely divisible); see, e.g.,
[
6
, Theorem 5]
. Consequently, with high probability,

\displaystyle\|EU\Lambda^{-1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\lesssim\frac{r^{1/2}\exp(c_{0}r^{\beta})}{n^{1/2}},

\displaystyle\|Q\|_{2\to\infty}\lesssim\frac{r^{-2\alpha}\exp(2c_{0}r^{\beta})\log^{1/2}{n}}{n\rho_{n}^{1/2}}+\frac{r^{(1-\alpha)}\exp(3c_{0}r^{\beta}/2)\log^{3/2}{n}}{n^{3/2}\rho_{n}}.

In particular, choosing
r=o((\log n\rho_{n})^{1/\beta})
yields
\|\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}^{1/2}W^{(n)}-U\Lambda^{1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}=o(\rho_{n}^{1/2})
with high probability, where
EU\Lambda^{-1/2}
is the dominant term. These findings are consistent with the results in
[
55
]
showing that
n\times n
matrices whose entries are of the form
h(x_{i},x_{j})
, where
h
is piecewise analytic and
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are bounded latent variables, can be well-approximated entrywise by a matrix of rank
O(\log n)
.
∎

(a)
Normalized eigenvalues
\lambda_{r}/n

(b)
Normalized eigenvalue gaps
(\lambda_{r}-\lambda_{r+1})/n

Figure 1:
Plots of the forty largest eigenvalues (left panel) and gap between consecutive eigenvalues (right panel) for
n\times n
edge probability matrices
P
when the link function is
\kappa(x,y)=\exp(-\|x-y\|)
, where
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are sampled i.i.d. from the uniform distribution on the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
n=8000,\rho_{n}=1
. There are
10
blue-colored lines (nearly indistinguishable) in each panel, one for each independent Monte Carlo replicate. The dashed line in the left panel corresponds to the best approximation of the form
\lambda_{r}/n\propto r^{-3/2}
.

Remark 7

(Large and small eigenvalue gaps)
.

For ease of exposition, our discussion in
Remarks

5
and
6
assumes that the gaps between consecutive eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
decay to zero at a given rate. However, in practice, while the eigenvalues themselves can exhibit a certain decay rate, gaps between these eigenvalues can be arbitrarily small. Indeed,
Fig.

1
presents a summary of the
40
largest eigenvalue of the
n\times n
matrix
P
for
n=8000
when the link function is
\kappa(x,y)=\exp(-\|x-y\|)
and the latent positions are sampled i.i.d. from the uniform distribution on the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. While the eigenvalues of
P
can be fitted quite well by a curve of the form
\lambda_{r}/n\propto r^{-3/2}
, the gaps between consecutive eigenvalues are generally near-zero except for visible jumps at a few locations, such as
r=1,4,9,15
.
∎

3.1
Extension to indefinite kernels

We now consider the case where
\kappa
is indefinite, for which the edge probability matrix
P
and the integral operator
\mathscr{K}
induced by
\kappa
per
Eq.

2.1
each have both positive and negative eigenvalues. The first issue we face is that the bound
\|U|\Lambda|^{1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\rho_{n}^{1/2}
for all
r
in
Eq.

2.2
no longer holds. Indeed, although

\|U|\Lambda|^{1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}^{2}=\|U|\Lambda|U_{\perp}^{\top}\|_{\max}\leq\|\,|P|\,\|_{2\to\infty},

here the entries of
U|\Lambda|U^{\top}
and
|P|\equiv(P^{2})^{1/2}
are not directly related to those of
P
. In other words, there is no closed-form expression for
|P|
in terms of simple element-wise operations on
P
when
P
is indefinite.
Nevertheless, from
Eq.

1.1
we at least have

\|U|\Lambda|^{1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\leq|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/2}\|U\Lambda\|_{2\to\infty}=|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/2}\|PU\|_{2\to\infty}\leq|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/2}n^{1/2}\rho_{n},

(3.14)

where the last inequality holds because
\|PU\|_{2\to\infty}=\|P\|_{2\to\infty}
whenever
U
has orthonormal columns, and the entries of
P
are bounded in magnitude by
\rho_{n}
. Since
|\lambda_{r}|\leq n\rho_{n}
, the bound in
Eq.

3.14
is worse than in
Eq.

2.2
, yet it is still meaningful
because it yields the following generalization of
Theorem

1
presented in
Theorem

2
.

Note that by convention, unless specified otherwise, the eigenvalues
\{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
of
A
and eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
of
P
are always ordered in decreasing modulus, that is,
|\widehat{\lambda}_{1}|\geq|\widehat{\lambda}_{2}|\geq\dots\geq 0
and
|\lambda_{1}|\geq|\lambda_{2}|\geq\dots\geq 0
.

Theorem 2

(Fine-grained eigenvector perturbation analysis for LPGs with general kernels)
.

Let
A\sim\mathrm{LPG}(\kappa,F;\rho_{n})
be a graph on
n
vertices generated according to
Definition

1
, where
\kappa
is possibly indefinite. For a given
r\geq 1
, let the diagonal matrices
\widehat{\Lambda}
and
\Lambda
contain the
r
largest in magnitude eigenvalues of
A
and
P
, respectively, and let
U
and
\widehat{U}
be the
n\times r
matrices whose orthonormal columns are the corresponding eigenvectors of
A
and
P
. Let
\delta_{r}=|\lambda_{r}|-|\lambda_{r+1}|
. Fix
\nu\geq 2
and suppose
r\geq 1
is chosen such that the conditions

\displaystyle\delta_{r}\geq\max\{4\varsigma(\nu,n),\tfrac{16}{3}(\nu+2)\log n\},

(3.15)

\displaystyle|\lambda_{r}|\geq\max\left\{16(\nu+2)\log n+\frac{64\vartheta(\nu,n)^{2}}{\delta_{r}},\sqrt{n\rho_{n}\vartheta(\nu+2,r,n)}\right\},

(3.16)

are both satisfied, where
\varsigma
and
\vartheta
are defined in
Eqs.

3.3
and
3.4
. Then, there exists an
r\times r
orthogonal matrix
W^{(n)}
such that

\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}W^{(n)}-U|\Lambda|^{1/2}=EU|\Lambda|^{-1/2}+Q,

(3.17)

where
EU|\Lambda|^{-1/2}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|EU|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\frac{11}{2}|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/2}\sqrt{\rho_{n}\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)}

(3.18)

with probability at least
1-n^{-\nu}
, while
Q
satisfies

\begin{split}\|Q\|_{2\to\infty}&\lesssim\frac{n^{3/2}\rho_{n}^{2}}{|\lambda_{r}|^{1/2}\delta_{r}^{2}}+\frac{(\rho_{n}\log n)^{1/2}}{|\lambda_{r}|^{1/2}}\left(\frac{(rn\rho_{n})^{1/2}+\log n}{\delta_{r}}\right)\end{split}

(3.19)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-\nu})
. Here,
\lesssim
and
O(\cdot)
hide universal constants that depend only on
\nu
but not on
r
,
n
,
\rho_{n}
, or
\kappa
.

Remark 8

(Residual analysis for positive semidefinite versus possibly indefinite kernels)
.

We now compare the terms appearing in the upper bound for
Q
in
Theorems

1
and
2
. To begin, observe that the second terms in each of
Eqs.

3.7
and
3.19
are identical. The first term in
Eq.

3.19
resembles the first term in
Eq.

3.7
, with the main difference being the additional multiplicative factor
(n\rho_{n})^{1/2}|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/2}
due to the use of
Eq.

3.14
as described above.
Eq.

3.14
also manifests itself in
Eq.

3.16
, where the former condition
\lambda_{r}\geq\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)
(see
Eq.

3.2
) is replaced with the more restrictive condition
|\lambda_{r}|\geq\sqrt{n\rho_{n}\vartheta(\nu+2,r,n)}
. The explanation for this is rooted in the main order term
EU|\Lambda|^{-1/2}
. More specifically, a modified version of Bernstein’s inequality (see
Eq.

B.14
) yields the bound

\|EU\Lambda^{-1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\leq 2\sqrt{2}\lambda_{r}^{-1/2}\sqrt{\rho_{n}\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)}+\tfrac{8}{3}\|U|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)

with probability at least
1-n^{-\nu}
. If we simplify the above display expression to keep only the first term as in
Eq.

3.18
, then we need to ensure that

\|U|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)\leq n^{1/2}\rho_{n}|\lambda_{r}|^{-3/2}\leq|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/2}\sqrt{\rho_{n}\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)}.

Ignoring constant factors, this is equivalent to requiring
|\lambda_{r}|\geq\sqrt{n\rho_{n}\vartheta(\nu+2,r,n)}
. Finally, similar to the discussion surrounding
Eq.

3.8
, with high probability
\|Q\|_{2\to\infty}=o(|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/2}\sqrt{\rho_{n}\vartheta(\nu+2,r,n)})
, so
EU|\Lambda|^{-1/2}
is the dominant term in the expansion of
Eq.

3.17
whenever the conditions

\displaystyle\delta_{r}^{4}(r+\log n)=\omega\left((n\rho_{n})^{3}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\delta_{r}=\omega\left(\max\left\{\sqrt{n\rho_{n}\log n},\log n\right\}\right)

(3.20)

are simultaneously satisfied.
∎

We pause here to discuss choosing
r\equiv r(n)
to grow with
n
in
Theorem

2
. For simplicity of presentation, assume that
\mathrm{rk}(\kappa)=\infty
, otherwise there exists a positive constant
M<\infty
for which
\mathrm{rk}(P)\leq M
almost surely and hence we are back in the more widely studied, comparatively easy finite-dimensional low-rank setting where we can simply choose
r=\mathrm{rk}(P)
for sufficiently large
n
. Recall that when
\kappa
is positive semidefinite, then we can apply the concentration inequality in
Eq.

2.5
to obtain a sequence
\{r(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
(possibly diverging with
n
) for which
Theorem

2
applies; see
Remarks

4
,
5
and
6
for additional details. In contrast, we are unaware of any prior result that yields a bound as sharp as
Eq.

2.5
when
\kappa
is indefinite.

To elaborate, since
\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}|\kappa(x,y)|\leq 1
, by
[
37
, Theorem 3.1]
we have

\delta_{2}\left((n\rho_{n})^{-1}\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k\geq 1},\{\mu_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\right)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}0

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where the distance
\delta_{2}
between sequences
(v_{k})_{k\geq 1}
and
(w_{k})_{k\geq 1}
can be written as

\delta_{2}(v,w)=\min_{\sigma}\sum_{k}(v_{k}-w_{\sigma(k)})^{2}

(3.21)

and the minimization is over all bijections
\sigma
of the natural integers. The upshot is that

Eq.

3.21
only guarantees that the extended spectrum of
P
, properly normalized, converges to that of
\mathscr{K}
but does not specify a convergence rate.

If we further assume that

\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}(\mu_{r}^{2}+\mu_{s}^{2})\int_{\mathcal{X}}\phi_{r}^{2}(x)\phi_{s}^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}F(x)=C_{*}<\infty,

(3.22)

then
[
37
, Corollary 4.3]
gives the bound

\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{2}^{2}\left((n\rho_{n})^{-1}\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k\geq 1},\{\mu_{k}\}_{k\geq 1})\right)\right]\leq 6C_{*}n^{-1}.

An application of Markov’s inequality thus yields a convergence rate of
O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
for
(n\rho_{n})^{-1}\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
(here
X_{n}=O_{p}(f(n))
means that for any
\epsilon>0
there exists a
C>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(X_{n}\leq Cf(n))\geq 1-\epsilon
).
While this bound is qualitatively similar to
Eq.

2.5
, there are two significant differences. Firstly,
Eq.

3.22
is difficult to verify in practice, since we only observe
A
while
\kappa
is unknown. Secondly, the rate
O(n^{-1/2})
only holds with probability
1-o(1)
, so we cannot guarantee that
\delta_{2}((n\rho_{n})^{-1}\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k\geq 1},\{\mu_{k}\}_{k\geq 1})=O(n^{-1/2})
asymptotically almost surely. This makes our goal of letting
r
grow with
n
more subtle. For example, suppose
|\mu_{k}|
exhibits polynomial decay and let
\mathcal{E}_{n}
be the event that
Eqs.

3.15
and
3.16
are satisfied simultaneously. Then, for a sequence
\{r(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
similar to that in
Remark

5
, we only have
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{n})=1
and not
\mathbb{P}(\cup_{m=1}^{\infty}\cap_{n=m}^{\infty}\mathcal{E}_{n})=1
.

On a related note,
[
38
, Definition 3]
considers the assumption

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h_{\kappa}(x)=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{r\geq 1}|\mu_{r}|\phi_{r}^{2}(x)<\infty.

(3.23)

We note that
Eq.

3.23
is somewhat easier to verify than
Eq.

3.22
. In particular, it is satisfied whenever
\mathscr{K}
has only a
finite
number of positive or negative eigenvalues; examples of kernels with this property are conditionally positive definite kernels. By applying
[
33
, Theorem 8.1.2]
, one can show that
\max_{k\geq 1}|(n\rho_{n})^{-1}\lambda_{k}-\mu_{k}|=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, with the convention that
\lambda_{k}=0
for
k>n
; see also
[
38
, Equation 16]
. Once again, here the rate of order
n^{-1/2}
only holds with probability
1-o(1)
, not with high probability as we desire.

Finally, under the conditions in
Eq.

3.23
, for any fixed but arbitrary
k\geq 1

[
56
, Theorem 1]
gives the bound

|(n\rho_{n})^{-1}\lambda_{k}-\mu_{k}|\lesssim|\mu_{k}|\sqrt{\frac{\mathcal{V}_{1}(R(k))\log(R(k)/\alpha)}{n}}

(3.24)

with probability at least
1-\alpha
. Here,
\mathcal{V}_{1}(i)=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{k=1}^{i}\phi_{k}^{2}(x)
, and
R(k)
is the minimum index
R
for which
|\mu_{k}|\geq\max\left\{\sum_{\ell>R}|\mu_{\ell}|,\sqrt{R\sum_{\ell>R}\mu_{\ell}^{2}}\right\}
. Although
Eq.

3.24
closely resembles
Eq.

2.5
, one still needs to bound
R(k)
and
\mathcal{V}_{1}(R(k))
. Doing so will require introducing further assumptions, as otherwise both the tail sequence of eigenvalues
\{\mu_{\ell}\}_{\ell>k}
and the eigenfunctions
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\leq k}
are unknown.

Finally, we end this subsection by presenting a result concerning the limiting distribution for the row-wise fluctuations of
\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}W^{(n)}-U|\Lambda|^{1/2}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
with
r
fixed.

Corollary 2

(Row-wise limiting distribution for
\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}
)
.

Assume the setting and notations in
Theorem

2
. Fix a
r\geq 1
such that the following condition is satisfied.

\delta_{r}=\omega\left(\max\left\{|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/2}\sqrt{n\rho_{n}}\log^{3/2}{n},|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/2}n\rho_{n}\sqrt{r\log n},|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/4}n\rho_{n}\right\}\right)

(3.25)

For a specified choice of index
i\in[n]
, define

\Sigma_{i}=|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\left(\sum_{k}p_{ik}(1-p_{ik})U_{k}U_{k}^{\top}\right)|\Lambda|^{-1/2},

(3.26)

and suppose that there exists a constant
c_{0}>0
such that

c_{0}\rho_{n}I\preceq\left(\sum_{k}p_{ik}(1-p_{ik})U_{k}U_{k}^{\top}\right)\preceq\rho_{n}I,

(3.27)

where
\preceq
denote the Loewner ordering for positive semidefinite matrices.
Then,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\left((W^{(n)})^{\top}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\widehat{U}_{i}-|\Lambda|^{1/2}U_{i}\right)\rightsquigarrow N(0,I)

(3.28)

as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Here,
\widehat{U}_{i}
and
U_{i}
are the
i
-th row of
\widehat{U}
and
U
, respectively. Furthermore, if
\kappa
is positive semidefinite, then
Eq.

3.25
can be weakened slightly to

\delta_{r}=\omega\left(\max\left\{\lambda_{r}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n\rho_{n}}\log^{3/2}{n},\lambda_{r}^{-1/2}n\rho_{n}\sqrt{r\log n},(n\rho_{n})^{3/4}\right\}\right).

(3.29)

Corollary

2
extends previous row-wise limiting distribution results for
\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}
under low-rank assumptions, where
\mathrm{rk}(P)=d
for some constant
d
(see e.g.,
[
60
,
49
,
4
]
), to the growing rank or full rank setting.
Eq.

3.26
provides an expression for the covariance matrix of
E_{i}U|\Lambda|^{-1/2}=|\Lambda|^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(a_{ik}-p_{ik})U_{k}
; here
E_{i}
is the
i
-th row of
E
.
Eq.

3.27
is satisfied whenever there exists a constant
c_{0}>0
such that
\kappa(x,x^{\prime})\in[c_{0},1-c_{0}]
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
.
Eq.

3.25
or
Eq.

3.29
ensure that
\|\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}Q_{i}\|\rightarrow 0
in probability, and we can ignore all terms depending on
Q
in the limiting distribution.
Eq.

3.28
then follows from applying the Lindeberg–Feller central limit theorem
[
57
, Proposition 2.27]
to
\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}E_{i}U|\Lambda|^{-1/2}
. Finally,
r
is fixed in
Corollary

2
as otherwise if
r\rightarrow\infty
then the convergence in distribution of
Eq.

3.28
is possibly not well-defined. Nevertheless, to handle growing
r
, we can replace the limiting distribution of
\xi_{i}=(W^{(n)})^{\top}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}\widehat{U}_{i}-|\Lambda|^{1/2}U_{i}
with that of
\|\xi_{i}\|
and then apply a comparison result for quadratic forms in
[
48
]
; see
Theorem

6
in
Section

A.8
for more details.

3.2
Estimation of
U
versus
U|\Lambda|^{1/2}
versus
U\Lambda

We next present perturbation expansions and two-to-infinity norm bounds for the expressions
\widehat{U}W^{(n)}-U
and
\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}W^{(n)}-U\Lambda
, where
W^{(n)}
denotes an orthogonal matrix. Although these quantities are closely related and themselves resemble
\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}W^{(n)}-U|\Lambda|^{1/2}
, certain inference tasks can be simplified if one chooses wisely from among these expressions. For example, if
\kappa
is positive semidefinite and
P_{r}=U\Lambda U^{\top}
denotes the best rank-
r
approximation to
P
, then it is reasonably straightforward to analyze the estimation error of
\widehat{P}_{r}=\widehat{Z}_{r}\widehat{Z}_{r}^{\top}
, where
\widehat{Z}_{r}=\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}^{1/2}
, using the results in
Theorem

1
. Similarly, in
Section

4.2
, we consider a test statistic based on the Euclidean norm row difference
\|(\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda})_{i}-(\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda})_{j}\|
for which it is more convenient to leverage the row-wise expansion for
\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}
given below.

Theorem 3

(Additional eigenvector perturbation analysis for LPGs with general kernels)
.

Assume the setting and notations in
Theorem

2
where
\kappa
is possibly indefinite. Then, there exists an
r\times r
orthogonal matrix
W^{(n)}
such that

\widehat{U}W^{(n)}-U=EU\Lambda^{-1}+\mathring{Q},

(3.30)

where
EU\Lambda^{-1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|EU\Lambda^{-1}\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\frac{11}{2}\rho_{n}^{1/2}|\lambda_{r}|^{-1}\sqrt{\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)}

(3.31)

with probability at least
1-n^{-\nu}
and
\mathring{Q}
satisfies

\begin{split}\|\mathring{Q}\|_{2\to\infty}&\lesssim\frac{n^{3/2}\rho_{n}^{2}}{|\lambda_{r}|\delta_{r}^{2}}+\frac{(\rho_{n}\log n)^{1/2}}{|\lambda_{r}|}\left(\frac{(rn\rho_{n})^{1/2}+\log n}{\delta_{r}}\right)\end{split}

(3.32)

with probability at least
1-O(n^{-\nu})
. Similarly, it holds that

\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}W^{(n)}-U\Lambda=EU+\breve{Q},

where
EU
and
\breve{Q}
now satisfy

\displaystyle\|EU\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\frac{11}{2}\rho_{n}^{1/2}\sqrt{\vartheta(\nu+1,r,n)},

(3.33)

\displaystyle\|\breve{Q}\|_{2\to\infty}\lesssim\frac{n^{3/2}\rho_{n}^{2}}{\delta_{r}^{2}}+(\rho_{n}\log n)^{1/2}\left(\frac{(rn\rho_{n})^{1/2}+\log n}{\delta_{r}}\right)

(3.34)

with probability at least
1-n^{-\nu}
and
1-O(n^{-\nu})
respectively.
Furthermore, if
\kappa
is positive semidefinite, then the above bound for
\mathring{Q}

simplifies to

\displaystyle\|\mathring{Q}\|_{2\to\infty}\lesssim\frac{n\rho_{n}^{3/2}}{\lambda_{r}^{1/2}\delta_{r}^{2}}+\frac{(\rho_{n}\log n)^{1/2}}{\lambda_{r}}\left(\frac{(rn\rho_{n})^{1/2}+\log n}{\delta_{r}}\right)

(3.35)

but the bound for
\breve{Q}
remains unchanged. Above, the notation
\lesssim
and
O(\cdot)
suppress universal constants that depend only on
\nu
but not on
r
,
n
,
\rho_{n}
, or
\kappa
.

The first term in the bound for
\mathring{Q}
corresponds to
\|U\|_{2\to\infty}\times\|U^{\top}\widehat{U}-(W^{(n)})^{\top}\|
. If
\kappa
is positive semidefinite, then
\|U\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\rho_{n}^{1/2}|\lambda_{r}|^{-1/2}
which yields the sharper bound in
Eq.

3.35
, while if
\kappa
is indefinite then we can only obtain
\|U\|_{2\to\infty}\leq n^{1/2}\rho_{n}|\lambda_{r}|^{-1}
as in
Eq.

3.32
. See also the discussion in
Remark

8
. Similarly, the first term in the bound for
\breve{Q}
corresponds to
\|U\Lambda\|_{2\to\infty}\times\|U^{\top}\widehat{U}-(W^{(n)})^{\top}\|
. However, in contrast to the case involving
\|U\|_{2\to\infty}
, we always have
\|U\Lambda\|_{2\to\infty}\leq n^{1/2}\rho_{n}
, and thus there are no differences in the upper bound for
\breve{Q}
when
\kappa
is positive semidefinite versus when it is indefinite.

Finally, we note that
Theorem

3
can be adapted to yields a deterministic perturbation bound for the
r
leading eigenvectors
U
of a symmetric matrix
M
when it is perturbed by some arbitrary noise matrix
E
; see
Theorem

5
in
Section

A.5
for a formal statement. Notably, the bound in
Theorem

5
only depend on quantities that are
linear
in
E
and thus, for many inference settings, can be analyzed using standard matrix perturbation and concentration inequalities.

3.3
Related works

We now compare our results with existing perturbation analysis and bounds in the literature. Notably, two-to-infinity norm bounds for
\widehat{U}-UW^{(n)}
have appeared in numerous publications to date; see
[
15
,
16
,
1
,
39
,
42
,
45
,
60
,
22
]
for an incomplete list of references. However, these works overwhelmingly either focus only on first order upper bounds for
\widehat{U}-UW^{(n)}
, which may not be sufficiently refined for inference purpose, or are restricted to the case where
\mathrm{rk}(P)\ll n
. In contrast, this paper delicately decomposes
\widehat{U}-UW^{(n)}
into the main order term
EU\Lambda^{-1}
and a residual order term
Q
while minimizing factors that depend on inverse powers of
\delta_{r}
. To illustrate these challenges, suppose that
\kappa
is positive semidefinite and consider the statement of
[
1
, Theorem 2.1]
. Assumption A.3 in
[
1
]
posits that
32\|P\|/\delta_{r}\max\{\gamma,\varphi(\gamma)\}\leq 1
and that
\|E\|\leq\gamma\delta_{r}
, where
\gamma
is any value for which
\|P\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\gamma\delta_{r}
and
\varphi(x)
is a function proportional to
1/(\max\{1,\log(x^{-1})\})
. For the setting in
Theorem

3
, we have
\|P\|\asymp n\rho_{n}
and
\|P\|_{2\to\infty}\asymp n^{1/2}\rho_{n}
, hence we can take
\gamma=\delta_{r}^{-1}(n\rho_{n})^{1/2}
. Theorem 2.1 in
[
1
]
then yields the high probability bound

\|\widehat{U}W^{(n)}-U\|_{2\to\infty}\leq\|EU\Lambda^{-1}\|_{2\to\infty}+\|\widetilde{Q}\|_{2\to\infty},

where
\widetilde{Q}
satisfies

\begin{split}\|\widetilde{Q}\|_{2\to\infty}&\lesssim\kappa_{r}(\kappa_{r}+\phi(1))(\gamma+\phi(\gamma))\|U\|_{2\to\infty}+\gamma\|P\|_{2\to\infty}\times\frac{1}{\delta_{r}}\\
&\lesssim\|U\|_{2\to\infty}\times\frac{\kappa_{r}^{2}(n\rho_{n})^{1/2}}{\delta_{r}}+\frac{n\rho_{n}^{3/2}}{\delta_{r}^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\frac{\kappa_{r}^{2}n^{1/2}\rho_{n}}{\lambda_{r}^{1/2}\delta_{r}}+\frac{n\rho_{n}^{3/2}}{\delta_{r}^{2}}.\end{split}

(3.36)

Here (slight abuse of notation),
\kappa_{r}=\lambda_{1}/\delta_{r}
, with
\kappa_{r}\rightarrow\infty
as
r\rightarrow\infty
.

Comparing
Eqs.

3.36
and
3.35
, we see that the first term in
Eq.

3.35
is
\lambda_{r}^{-1/2}
times smaller than the last term in
Eq.

3.36
. Meanwhile, the ratio of the second term in
Eq.

3.35
to that of the second to last term in
Eq.

3.36
is

\left(\frac{\kappa_{r}^{2}n^{1/2}\rho_{n}}{\lambda_{r}^{1/2}\delta_{r}}\right)^{-1}\frac{(\rho_{n}\log n)^{1/2}}{\lambda_{r}}\left(\frac{\log n+(rn\rho_{n})^{1/2}}{\delta_{r}}\right)=\frac{(r\log n)^{1/2}}{\kappa_{r}^{2}}\left(\frac{\log n}{\lambda_{r}^{1/2}(n\rho_{n})^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{r}^{1/2}}\right),

which also converges to zero at the rate
O(\kappa_{r}^{-2}\lambda_{r}^{-1/2})
(ignoring logarithmic factors). As such, the bound in
Eq.

3.35
is smaller than in

Eq.

3.36
by a multiplicative factor of at least
\lambda_{r}^{1/2}
.
Furthermore, for
Eq.

3.36
, to guarantee that
\widetilde{Q}
is negligible compared to
EU\Lambda^{-1}
, we would need to require that simultaneously

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}^{-1}(r\rho_{n}\log n)^{1/2}=\omega(n\rho_{n}^{3/2}\delta_{r}^{-2})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\delta_{r}^{2}(r\log n)^{1/2}=\omega(n\rho_{n}\lambda_{r}),

(3.37)

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}^{-1}(r\rho_{n}\log n)^{1/2}=\omega(\kappa_{r}^{2}n^{1/2}\rho_{n}/(\lambda_{r}^{1/2}\delta_{r}))\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\delta_{r}^{2}(r\log n)=\omega(\kappa_{r}^{4}n\rho_{n}\lambda_{r}).

(3.38)

However, the conditions in
Eqs.

3.37
and
3.38
might both be infeasible when
n
increases as
\kappa_{r}>1
and
n\rho_{n}\geq\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{r}\geq\delta_{r}
always. In contrast, our bounds in
Theorem

3
imply that
\|\mathring{Q}\|_{2\to\infty}
is negligible compared to
\|EU\Lambda^{-1}\|_{2\to\infty}
whenever

r\delta_{r}^{4}\log n=\omega((n\rho_{n})^{2}\lambda_{r}),

(3.39)

which is a much milder condition and can always be satisfied for any given
r
as
n
increases.

Next,
[
39
, Theorem 3.4]
improves upon
[
1
, Theorem 2.1]
and yields an upper bound for
\widetilde{Q}
of the form

\|\widetilde{Q}\|_{2\to\infty}\lesssim\frac{n^{1/2}\rho_{n}}{\lambda_{r}^{1/2}\delta_{r}}+\frac{\rho_{n}^{1/2}(r^{1/2}+\log^{1/2}{n})}{\lambda_{r}}\times\frac{(n\rho_{n})^{1/2}(r+\log n)^{3/4}}{\delta_{r}}.

(3.40)

Comparing
Eqs.

3.35
and
3.40
we see that our
bound in
Eq.

3.35
is still of smaller order. More
importantly, however, is that to guarantee
\widetilde{Q}
negligible
compared to
EU\Lambda^{-1}
in
Eq.

3.40
one needs

\delta_{r}(r^{1/2}+\log^{1/2}{n})=\omega((n\rho_{n})^{1/2}\lambda_{r}^{1/2})

(3.41)

which, similar to the conditions in

Eqs.

3.37
and
3.38
, may be infeasible as
n

increases. Given that our inference tasks generally require choosing

r
to grow with
n
, the ability to leverage less stringent
conditions (such as
Eq.

3.39
) leads to a faster growth
rate for
r
which then yields more accurate estimation of
P
in

Section

4.1
and larger asymptotic power for
testing equality of latent positions in
Section

4.2
.

Next, we compare our results with those in
[
38
]
wherein
the author considered the notion of graph root distribution. More
specifically, let
\kappa
be a graphon (equivalently, a symmetric
measurable function from
[0,1]^{2}
to
[0,1]
), and suppose the
integral operator associated with
\kappa
(see
Eq.

2.1
)
satisfies
Eq.

3.23
. Let
\xi_{1}\geq\xi_{2}\geq\dots>0
be the enumeration of the positive eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
,
and, with a slight abuse of notations, let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots

denote the corresponding eigenfunctions. Similarly, let
\zeta_{1}\geq\zeta_{2}\geq\dots>0
be the enumeration of the
moduli

of the negative eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
, and let
\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\dots
denote the corresponding eigenfunctions. Suppose also
that there exists positive numbers
c_{1}\leq c_{2}
,
1<\alpha\leq\beta
such that for all
j\geq 1

c_{1}j^{-\alpha}\leq\min\{\xi_{j},\zeta_{j}\}\leq\max\{\xi_{j},\zeta_{j}\}\leq c_{2}j^{-\alpha},\quad\text{and}\quad\min\{\xi_{j}-\xi_{j+1},\zeta_{j}-\zeta_{j+1}\}\geq c_{1}j^{-\beta};

see
[
38
, Assumption A2]
. If

n\rho_{n}\rightarrow\infty
and
r=o(\min\{n^{1/(2\beta+\alpha)},(n\rho_{n})^{1/(2\beta)}\})
, then by
[
38
, Theorem 4.5]
we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}-U|\Lambda|^{1/2}\|_{F}=O_{p}\left(r^{(\beta-\alpha+1)/2}\right),

(3.42)

\displaystyle n^{-1}\|\rho_{n}^{-1/2}\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}-Z_{r}\|_{F}^{2}=O_{p}\left(r^{2\beta-\alpha+1}(n\rho_{n})^{-1}+n^{-(\alpha-1)/(2\beta)}+r^{2\beta+1}n^{-1}\right).

(3.43)

Here,
\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}
and
U|\Lambda|^{1/2}
are now
n\times 2r
matrices whose columns are the scaled eigenvectors of
A
and
P
, corresponding to the
r
largest positive eigenvalues and
r
largest (in modulus) negative eigenvalues, while
Z_{r}
is the
n\times 2r
matrix with rows of the form

\left(\sqrt{\xi_{1}}\phi_{1}(X_{i}),\sqrt{\xi_{2}}\phi_{2}(X_{i}),\dots,\sqrt{\xi_{r}}\phi_{r}(X_{i}),\sqrt{\zeta_{1}}\psi_{1}(X_{i}),\dots,\sqrt{\zeta_{r}}\psi_{r}(X_{i})\right).

Above, the notation
O_{p}(\cdot)
denotes a big-Oh bound that holds with probability converging to one as
n\rightarrow\infty
and where, for ease of exposition, we have ignored all orthogonal transformations
W
in the bounds. In contrast, applying our
Theorem

2
to the current setting yields

\begin{split}\|\widehat{U}|\widehat{\Lambda}|^{1/2}-U|\Lambda|^{1/2}\|_{2\to\infty}&\lesssim\frac{r^{\alpha/2}(r^{1/2}+\log^{1/2}{n})}{n^{1/2}}+\frac{r^{2\beta+\alpha/2}+r^{\beta+(\alpha+1)/2}\log^{1/2}{n}}{n\rho_{n}^{1/2}}\\
&\qquad+\frac{r^{\beta+\alpha/2}\log^{3/2}{n}}{n^{3/2}\rho_{n}}\end{split}

with high probability. The first term in our
2\to\infty
norm bound is roughly of order
n^{-1/2}
times smaller than the corresponding Frobenius norm error in
Eq.

3.42
; the extra
\log^{1/2}{n}
term can be attributed to the bound holding with non-asymptotic high probability as opposed to with probability converging to one. Meanwhile the remaining terms in our
2\to\infty
norm bound have denominators that are of order larger than
n^{1/2}
and can be made negligible for appropriate choices of
r
, e.g.,
r=(n\rho_{n})^{1/(4\beta-2)}
.

Our results do not include bounds of the form in
Eq.

3.43
, as our paper is mainly concerned with the row-wise difference of the leading eigenvectors of
A
compared to that of
P
, while
Eq.

3.43
is, intrinsically, about the difference between the leading eigenvectors of
P
compared to the
eigenfunctions
of
\mathscr{K}
. Nevertheless, if
\kappa
is positive semidefinite, then we can apply the ideas in
Remark

10
below to also obtain
2\to\infty
norm bounds for
\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}^{1/2}-Z_{r}
, where now both matrices are of size
n\times r
as
\mathscr{K}
cannot have negative eigenvalues. The case when
\kappa
is indefinite is more complicated and we leave it for future investigation.

Finally, we note that our results explicitly allow for repeated eigenvalues in both
P
and
\mathscr{K}
. In contrast,
[
38
]
requires a gap of order at least
j^{-\beta}
between the
j
-th and
j+1
-th eigenvalue for all
j\geq 1
. While
[
38
]
mentioned that repeated eigenvalues can be handled using the arguments therein, they did not explicitly do so, and to do so would require significant additional technicalities. Nevertheless, we believe that precise statements of results under the repeated eigenvalues setting are necessary in order to make them more widely applicable, especially in the infinite rank setting. Indeed, as
Remark

7
clearly shows, it is only meaningful to make assumptions about the rate of decay of eigenvalues but not their gaps.

4
Implications for inference

4.1
Entrywise bound for
P

We now apply the results in
Section

3
to obtain entrywise error bounds for estimating the edge probability matrix
P
.
Note that, for ease of exposition, the conditions on
r
are stated in terms of the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
of
P
. In practice, we can reformulated these conditions in terms of the eigenvalues
\{\widehat{\lambda}_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
of
A
. The details are straightforward (see e.g.,
Corollary

1
) and thus omitted.

Corollary 3
.

Assume the setting and notations in
Theorem

2
. Also, suppose
r
is chosen so that the conditions in

Eq.

3.20
are satisfied.
Define
\widehat{P}_{r}=\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}\widehat{U}^{\top}
and
P_{r}=U\Lambda U^{\top}
.
Then

\displaystyle\rho_{n}^{-1}\|\widehat{P}_{r}-P\|_{\max}\leq\rho_{n}^{-1}\|P_{r}-P\|_{\max}+O\left(|\lambda_{r}|^{-1}(n\rho_{n})^{1/2}(r^{1/2}+\log^{1/2}{n}\right),

(4.1)

which holds with high probability. Furthermore, if
\kappa
is positive semidefinite then, under the conditions in
Eq.

3.8
, the above bound can be improved to

\displaystyle\rho_{n}^{-1}\|\widehat{P}_{r}-P\|_{\max}\leq\rho_{n}^{-1}\|P_{r}-P\|_{\max}+O\left(\lambda_{r}^{-1/2}(r^{1/2}+\log^{1/2}n)\right),

(4.2)

which holds with high probability.

We now compare
Eqs.

4.2
and
4.1
with the results in
[
61
]
for
\|\widehat{P}_{r}-P\|_{F}
. Using the notations in the present paper,
[
61
, Theorem 1]
establishes that with high probability,

(n\rho_{n})^{-2}\|\widehat{P}_{r}-P\|_{F}^{2}\leq\frac{C_{1}r}{n\rho_{n}}+\frac{C_{2}}{(n\rho_{n})^{2}}\sum_{k>r}\lambda_{k}^{2},

(4.3)

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are positive constants depending on the eigenvalue threshold for choosing
r
in universal singular value thresholding (USVT)
[
17
,
61
]
. The first term on the right hand side of
Eq.

4.3
is due to the effect of estimating
\widehat{P}_{r}
by
P_{r}
and is analogous to the terms
O(\lambda_{r}^{-1}(r+\log n))
and
O(\lambda_{r}^{-2}n\rho_{n}(r+\log n)
obtained by squaring the second term in
Eqs.

4.2
and
4.1
, respectively, with the main differences being either the use of
\lambda_{r}^{-1}
or
\lambda_{r}^{-2}n\rho_{n}
in place of
(n\rho_{n})^{-1}
. These differences are attributable to the fact that
(n\rho_{n})^{-2}\|\widehat{P}_{r}-P_{r}\|_{F}^{2}
quantifies the
average
squared entrywise error between
\widehat{P}_{r}
and
P
, whereas the second term in
Eqs.

4.2
and
4.1
is for the
maximum
entrywise errors. Indeed, the factors
\lambda_{r}^{-1}
and
\lambda_{r}^{-2}n\rho_{n}
appear due to the fact that although
\|U\|=1
, the individual rows of
U
can be heterogeneous, and
\lambda_{r}/(n\rho_{n})
(
\lambda_{r}^{2}/(n\rho_{n})^{2}
, resp.) serves as a measure for the degree of heterogeneity between different rows of
U
when
P
is positive semidefinite (indefinite, resp.). Finally, the term
C_{2}(n\rho_{n})^{-2}\sum_{k>r}\lambda_{k}^{2}
in
Eq.

4.3
is an upper bound for
(n\rho_{n})^{-2}\|P_{r}-P\|_{F}^{2}
and serves as the analogue of
\rho_{n}^{-1}\|P_{r}-P\|_{\max}
in
Eqs.

4.2
and
4.1
.

Remark 9

(On edge probability estimation in network analysis)
.

There is a sizable existing literature on the estimation of
P
for independent edge random graphs. See
[
59
,
63
,
17
,
3
,
36
,
44
,
29
,
61
,
64
,
46
]
for an incomplete list of references. In particular, the two most common approaches are (i) fitting, via either (penalized) least squares or maximum likelihood estimation, a stochastic blockmodel (SBM) to the adjacency matrix
A

[
59
,
36
,
29
,
3
,
44
]
and (ii) (universal) singular value thresholding (USVT)
[
61
,
17
,
63
]
. It has been noted that, under certain conditions on the sparsity of the graphs, estimators obtained by fitting SBMs have Frobenius norm errors that are minimax optimal over the class of Hölder-continuous link functions
\kappa
on
[0,1]\times[0,1]
; see for example
[
29
,
36
]
or
[
61
, Appendix A]
. These estimators, however, can be computationally infeasible as their running time can be exponential in
n
. In contrast, estimators based on singular value thresholding, and thus (low rank) matrix factorizations, have running time of order
O(n^{3})
. Furthermore, while the Frobenius norm error of USVT estimates are not minimax optimal for the class of Hölder-continuous
\kappa
,
[
41
]
showed that they are nevertheless optimal for stochastic blockmodels among the class of all low-degree polynomial estimators. Subsequently,
[
18
]
showed that the Frobenius norm error of USVT is, up to a logarithmic factor, also minimax optimal among all
\kappa
for which
|\mu_{k}|\lesssim k^{-\alpha}
for some
\alpha\geq 1
and any
k\geq 1
. In other words, USVT is minimax optimal whenever the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
have polynomial or exponential rate of decay.

Our results presented in the current section provide further compelling evidence for the use of USVT, particularly since it leads to entrywise bounds and, potentially, confidence intervals for
P
(see
Remarks

10
and
11
). Both of these results are currently not available for other type of estimators due to the fact that Frobenius norm error bounds are not suitable for inferring row-wise or entrywise behavior of estimates.
∎

Remark 10

(Entrywise bounds and rates of convergence)
.

If
\kappa
is continuous and positive semidefinite, then we also have the following bound for
\rho_{n}^{-1}\|P_{r}-P\|_{\max}
.

Proposition 2
.

Consider the setting in
Theorem

1
where
\kappa
is a continuous, positive semidefinite kernel and suppose
\mu_{r}>\mu_{r+1}
. Then

\begin{split}\rho_{n}^{-1}\|P_{r}-P\|_{\max}&\lesssim\frac{\log^{1/2}{n}}{n^{1/2}(\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1})}+\max_{ij}\left|\sum_{k>r}\mu_{k}\phi_{k}(X_{i})\phi_{k}(X_{j})\right|\end{split}

(4.4)

with high probability.

The first term on the right hand side of
Eq.

4.4
converges to zero as
r\to\infty
, provided that
\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}=\omega(n^{-1/2}\log^{1/2}{n})
. As the only accumulation point for the
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
is at zero, such a sequence for
r
always exists. The second term also converges to zero due to the uniform convergence in Mercer’s theorem (see, e.g.,
[
51
, Theorem 4.49]
) but there is no
a priori
explicit convergence rate unless we put additional assumptions on
\kappa
. For example, if
\{X_{i}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\kappa
is
m
times continuously differentiable, then

\left|\max_{ij}\sum_{k>r}\mu_{k}\phi_{k}(X_{i})\phi_{k}(X_{j})\right|\lesssim\left(\sum_{k>r}\mu_{k}^{2}\right)^{m/(2m+2d)}.

See
[
52
, Theorem 1.2]
for more details. As a related example, if
\mu_{r}=O(r^{-\alpha})
and
\|\phi_{r}\|_{\infty}=O(r^{\beta})
for constants
\alpha\geq 2\beta+1>0
then
[
43
, Theorem 1]

yields the high probability bound

\rho_{n}^{-1}\|P_{r}-P\|_{\max}=O\left(n^{-(\alpha-1)/\alpha}\log n\right).

Combining
Eqs.

4.2
and
4.4
together with the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives

\rho_{n}^{-1}\|\widehat{P}_{r}-P\|_{\max}\lesssim\frac{r^{1/2}+\log^{1/2}{n}}{\lambda_{r}^{1/2}}+\frac{\log^{1/2}{n}}{n^{1/2}(\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1})}+\sup_{x}\sum_{k>r}\mu_{k}\phi_{k}^{2}(x).

Of note, the first term in the above bound depends on both
n
and graph sparsity
\rho_{n}
(through
\lambda_{r}
), the second term depends on
n
and the eigenvalue gap
\mu_{r}-\mu_{r+1}
but not on
\rho_{n}
, and the last term is independent of
n
.
∎

Remark 11

(Towards entrywise normality and inference)
.

Normal approximations and confidence intervals for the entries of
\widehat{P}_{r}
as estimates for
P
are much more difficult to derive in the current setting compared to when
P
is low-rank; see
[
20
, Theorems 4.10 and 4.11]
for low-rank examples. More specifically, assume for simplicity that
\kappa
is positive semidefinite. Then, using a similar derivations as in
Eq.

A.63
, we have

\rho_{n}^{-1}(\widehat{P}_{r}-P)=\rho_{n}^{-1}(A-P)UU^{\top}+\rho_{n}^{-1}UU^{\top}(A-P)+\rho_{n}^{-1}(P_{r}-P)+R,

where
R
is a residual matrix. Let
M=UU^{\top}
and denote by
e_{ij}
and
m_{ij}
the
ij
-th entry of
E=A-P
and
M
, respectively. Let
\zeta_{ij}
be the
ij
-th element of
EUU^{\top}+UU^{\top}E
. Then, for
i\not=j
, we have

\begin{split}\rho_{n}^{-1}(\zeta_{ij}+\zeta_{ji})&=\rho_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k\not=j}e_{ik}m_{kj}+\rho_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k\not=i}e_{kj}m_{ik}+\rho_{n}^{-1}e_{ij}(m_{ii}+m_{jj}),\end{split}

which, conditioning on
P
, is a sum of independent mean zero random variables. Next, define

\sigma_{ij}^{2}=\rho_{n}^{-2}\left(\sum_{k}[\,p_{ik}(1-p_{ik})m_{kj}^{2}+p_{kj}(1-p_{kj})m_{ik}^{2}]+2p_{ij}(1-p_{ij})m_{ii}m_{jj}\right).

(4.5)

Then, for a fixed
(i,j)
pair, we can apply the Lindeberg–Feller central limit theorem to show

\displaystyle\sigma_{ij}^{-1}\zeta_{ij}\rightsquigarrow N(0,1)\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Suppose there exists a constant
c_{0}>0
such that
\kappa(x,y)\geq c_{0}
for all
(x,y)\in\Omega\times\Omega
. Then,

\sigma_{ij}^{2}\geq\rho_{n}^{-2}\times\rho_{n}c_{0}(1-\rho_{n}c_{0})(m_{jj}+m_{ii}),

and
\sigma_{ij}
converges to zero at rate
\rho_{n}^{-1/2}\|U\|_{2\to\infty}\asymp\lambda_{r}^{-1/2}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Furthermore, there are also plug-in estimators
\widehat{\sigma}_{ij}
for which
\widehat{\sigma}_{ij}/\sigma_{ij}\rightarrow 1
in probability so that, by Slutsky’s theorem, we can replace with
\widehat{\sigma}_{ij}
in the above distributional convergence.
In contrast, the
ij
-th entry of
P_{r}-P
induces a bias term
b_{ij}=p^{(r)}_{ij}-p_{ij}
which, from the discussion in
Remark

10
, converges to zero but at a possibly arbitrarily slow rate. In other words, while we have a normal
approximation of the form

\sigma_{ij}^{-1}(\widehat{p}^{(r)}_{ij}+b_{ij}-p_{ij})\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0,1)\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty,

(4.6)

it might not always lead to meaningful inference results. For example, we cannot directly apply
Eq.

4.6
to construct confidence interval for
b_{ij}
due to the unknown bias term
b_{ij}
. Furthermore, while we can try to estimate
b_{ij}
, it is not clear if
\sigma_{ij}^{-1}|\widehat{b}_{ij}-b_{ij}|\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Indeed, as
P
is unknown, we can only estimate each
individual

b_{ij}
using the adjacency matrix
A
but since
b_{ij}
depends on the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
P

not in

U
and
\Lambda
, we cannot directly control their estimation errors using the results in the current paper.
We thus leave the open question of constructing confidence intervals for
p_{ij}
to future work.
∎

4.2
Testing for equality of latent positions

We now consider the problem of determining whether or not two given vertices
i
and
j
have the same latent positions. In other words, we are interested in testing the hypotheses

\mathbb{H}_{0}:X_{i}=X_{j}\quad\text{ versus }\quad\mathbb{H}_{1}:X_{i}\neq X_{j}.

(4.7)

This problem had been previously studied in
[
27
,
25
]
under the simpler setting wherein
P
is low-rank, i.e., when the kernel
\kappa
is a finite-rank kernel of the form
\kappa(X_{i},X_{j})=X_{i}^{\top}MX_{j}
for some
d\times d
symmetric matrix
M
. The authors of
[
27
,
25
]
showed that, with
r=d
, the test statistic based on the Mahalanobis distance between
\widehat{U}_{i}
and
\widehat{U}_{j}
(i.e., the
i
and
j
-th row of
\widehat{U}
) converges to a central
\chi^{2}
(non-central
\chi^{2}
, resp.) with
r
degrees of freedom under the null hypothesis (local alternative hypothesis, resp.). As the Mahalanobis distance is invariant with respect to invertible transformations, the same limiting distribution holds for the difference between
\widehat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\widehat{U}_{i}
and
\Lambda^{1/2}\widehat{U}_{j}
.

We now extend these results to the setting of our paper by allowing
r
to grow with
n
. Our test statistic, however, will be based on the
Euclidean distance
between
\widehat{\Lambda}\widehat{U}_{i}
and
\widehat{\Lambda}\widehat{U}_{j}
, as opposed to either form of the Mahalonobis distance described above. The rationale for using the Euclidean distance will be clarified in
Remark

14
.

Let
\widehat{\xi}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
be the
i
-th row of
\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}
and
U\Lambda
, respectively. Fix an arbitrary pair
\{i,j\}
and suppose that
X_{i}=X_{j}
. Then,
\xi_{i}=\xi_{j}
, and by
Theorem

3
, we have

\begin{split}W^{\top}(\widehat{\xi}_{i}-\widehat{\xi}_{j})=(E_{i}-E_{j})U+\gamma_{ij}\end{split}

for some
r\times r
orthogonal matrix
W
, where
\gamma_{ij}
is a residual lower-order term while
E_{i}
and
E_{j}
are the
i
-th and
j
-th row of
A-P
, respectively.
We then have

\begin{split}\|\widehat{\xi}_{i}-\widehat{\xi}_{j}\|^{2}=\|W^{\top}(\widehat{\xi}_{i}-\widehat{\xi}_{j})\|^{2}=(1+o(1))\|(E_{i}-E_{j})U\|^{2}=(1+o(1))\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}UU^{\top}\mathcal{D}\zeta,\end{split}

(4.8)

where
\zeta
is now a vector whose components are independent sub-Gaussian random variables with mean zero and variance one, and
\mathcal{D}
is a
n\times n
diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are
d_{k}=(2p_{ik}(1-p_{ik}))^{1/2}
for
1\leq k\leq n
. For notational simplicity, we have dropped the indices
\{i,j\}
from the matrix
\mathcal{D}
.
The following theorem establishes that
\|\widehat{\xi}_{i}-\widehat{\xi}_{j}\|
, properly translated and scaled, converges in distribution to a weighted sum of independent
\chi^{2}_{1}
random variables under the null hypothesis.

Theorem 4

(Asymptotic null distribution for testing equality of latent positions)
.

Let
A\sim\mathrm{LPG}(\kappa,F;\rho_{n})
be a graph on
n
vertices generated according to
Definition

1
with sparsity
\rho_{n}
. Denote the associated
latent positions by
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
. Set
r=r(n)
to be any positive integer such that
\delta_{r}=\omega(\sqrt{rn\rho_{n}\log n})
. Let
\widehat{U}
and
U
denote the
n\times r
matrices whose orthonormal columns are the eigenvectors corresponding to the
r
largest in magnitude eigenvalues of
A
and
P
, respectively. Let
M^{*}=\mathcal{D}UU^{\top}\mathcal{D}
, where
\mathcal{D}
is a diagonal matrix with diagonal entries

d_{k}=(p_{ik}(1-p_{ik})+p_{jk}(1-p_{jk}))^{1/2},\quad\text{for }1\leq k\leq n.

Then, under the null hypothesis,
\mathbb{H}_{0}:X_{i}=X_{j}
in
Eq.

4.7
, it holds that

\frac{\|(A\widehat{U})_{i}-(A\widehat{U})_{j}\|^{2}-\mathrm{tr}\,M^{*}}{\|M^{*}\|_{F}}=\frac{1}{\|M^{*}\|_{F}}\sum_{s=1}^{r}(Z_{s}^{2}-1)\lambda_{s}(M^{*})+\epsilon_{n},

(4.9)

where
\epsilon_{n}\rightarrow 0
in probability as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and
Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots,Z_{r}
are i.i.d. standard normals.

Furthermore, if
\kappa
is an infinite rank kernel, then
r(n)\rightarrow\infty
in probability as
n\rightarrow\infty
and

\frac{\|(A\widehat{U})_{i}-(A\widehat{U})_{j}\|^{2}-\mathrm{tr}\,M^{*}}{\|M^{*}\|_{F}}\rightsquigarrow N(0,2)\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(4.10)

Eqs.

4.9
and
4.10
yield large-sample approximations for
\|(A\widehat{U})_{i}-(A\widehat{U})_{j}\|^{2}
as a weighted sum of independent
\chi^{2}_{1}
random variables, provided that we can find estimates for
\mathrm{tr}\,M^{*}
and
\|M^{*}\|_{F}
. The following lemma illustrates one valid approach for doing so.

Lemma 1

(Consistent estimation of centering and scaling terms)
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
a be diagonal matrix with diagonal entries

\widehat{d}_{k}=|a_{ik}-a_{jk}|\quad\text{for }1\leq k\leq n.

Suppose
r
is chosen such that

\delta_{r}=\omega\left(\max\left\{\log^{3/2}{n},\sqrt{rn\rho_{n}\log n},(n\rho_{n})^{3/4}/(r^{1/4}+\log^{1/4}n)\right\}\right)

(4.11)

is satisfied.
Next, define

\widehat{\theta}=\mathrm{tr}\,\widehat{U}^{\top}\,\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{2}\widehat{U}=\|\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\widehat{U}\|_{F}^{2},\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\sigma}=\|\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\widehat{U}\widehat{U}^{\top}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\|_{F}.

Then, under the setting of
Theorem

4
together with the condition in
Eq.

4.11
, we have

\frac{\widehat{\theta}-\mathrm{tr}\,M^{*}}{\|M^{*}\|_{F}}\overset{\operatorname{p}}{\longrightarrow}0,\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\widehat{\sigma}}{\|M^{*}\|_{F}}\overset{\operatorname{p}}{\longrightarrow}1,\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Remark 12

(Selection of the embedding dimension
r
)
.

We remark that the condition for
r
in
Eq.

4.11
is more stringent than that in
Theorem

4
. The additional constraint manifests itself when we apply
Theorem

2
or
Theorem

3
to estimate
U
as it allows us to guarantee that the residual terms
\mathring{Q}
can be ignored. Although we can in principle relax
Eq.

4.11
to only require
\delta_{r}=\omega(\sqrt{rn\rho_{n}\log n})
, this weaker condition will require a more delicate analysis of
\|\widehat{U}^{\top}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{2}-\mathcal{D}^{2})\widehat{U}\|_{F}
. More specifically, if we replace
\widehat{U}
by some quantity
\widetilde{U}\approx\widehat{U}
that is independent of
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
, then we can bound
\|\widetilde{U}^{\top}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{2}-\mathcal{D}^{2})\widetilde{U}\|_{F}
via Bernstein’s inequality. As
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
depends only on the
i
-th and
j
-th row of
P
, a natural candidate for
\widetilde{U}
would be based on a leave-two-out analysis that replaces the entries in the
i
-th and
j
-th rows (as well as those in the
i
-th and
j
-th columns) of
A
with independent copies thereof. The technical details, while potentially interesting, are also rather tedious. Since our primary objective in
Lemma

1
is to present simple estimators for
\mathrm{tr}\,M_{*}
and
\|M_{*}\|_{F}
, we leave proving
Lemma

1
under weaker conditions to the interested reader.
∎

Combining
Theorems

4
and
1
yields the following testing procedure for the hypothesis testing problem in
Eq.

4.7

Notably, the proposed test statistic only involves
A
.

Corollary 4

(Testing equality of latent positions with data-driven rank selection)
.

Consider the setting in
Theorem

4
. Let
\widehat{r}=\widehat{r}(n)
be defined as

\widehat{r}=\argmax\Bigl\{j\colon|\widehat{\lambda}_{j}|-|\widehat{\lambda}_{j+1}|\geq\max\Bigl(\log^{7/4}(n),j^{1/2}\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{ave}}(A)}\log^{3/4}{n},(d_{\mathrm{ave}}(A))^{3/4}\Bigr)\Bigr\},

(4.12)

where
d_{\mathrm{ave}}(A)=n^{-1}\sum_{i}\sum_{j}a_{ij}
is the average degree of
A
. Denote by
\widehat{U}
the
n\times\widehat{r}
matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors corresponding to the
\widehat{r}
largest eigenvalues of
A
. For any
i\neq j
, define the test statistic

\displaystyle T(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})=\frac{\|(A_{i}-A_{j})^{\top}\widehat{U}\|^{2}-\|\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\widehat{U}\|_{F}^{2}}{\|\widehat{U}^{\top}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{2}\widehat{U}\|_{F}}=\frac{\sum_{k\not=\ell}(a_{ik}-a_{jk})(a_{i\ell}-a_{j\ell})\widehat{U}_{k}^{\top}\widehat{U}_{\ell}}{\bigl(\sum_{k}\sum_{\ell}(a_{ik}-a_{jk})^{2}(a_{i\ell}-a_{j\ell})^{2}(\widehat{U}_{k}^{\top}\widehat{U}_{\ell})^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}},

where
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
are the
i
-th and
j
-th columns of
A
, respectively, and where
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
is the diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
\widehat{d}_{k}=a_{ik}-a_{jk}
. Then, under the null hypothesis,
\mathbb{H}_{0}:X_{i}=X_{j}
in
Eq.

4.7
, we have

T(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})=\frac{1}{\|U^{\top}\mathcal{D}^{2}U\|_{F}}\sum_{s=1}^{\widehat{r}}(Z_{s}^{2}-1)\lambda_{s}(U^{\top}\mathcal{D}^{2}U)+\epsilon_{n},

(4.13)

where
\epsilon_{n}\to 0
in probability as
n\rightarrow\infty
,
\mathcal{D}^{2}
is the diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
d_{k}=2p_{ik}(1-p_{ik})
for
1\leq k\leq n
,
U
is the
n\times\widehat{r}
matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors corresponding to the
\widehat{r}
largest eigenvalues of
P
, and
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{\widehat{r}}
are independent
N(0,1)
random variables.

Furthermore, the sequence
\widehat{r}=\widehat{r}(n)
for
n\geq 1
is adaptive to the rank of
\kappa
, i.e.,

1.

If
\mathrm{rk}(\kappa)=r^{*}<\infty
, then as
n\to\infty
, both
\widehat{r}\rightarrow r^{*}
in probability
and

T(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})\rightsquigarrow\frac{1}{\|U_{r^{*}}^{\top}\mathcal{D}^{2}U_{r^{*}}\|_{F}}\sum_{s=1}^{r^{*}}(Z_{s}^{2}-1)\lambda_{s}(U_{r^{*}}^{\top}\mathcal{D}^{2}U_{r^{*}}),

(4.14)

where
U_{r^{*}}
is the
n\times r^{*}
matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors corresponding to the non-zero eigenvalues of
P
.

2.

If
\mathrm{rk}(\kappa)=\infty
, then as
n\to\infty
, both
\widehat{r}\rightarrow\infty
in probablity and

T(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})\rightsquigarrow N(0,2).

(4.15)

Remark 13

(Rank adaptivity of test statistic)
.

The test statistic
T(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})
in
Corollary

4
is, up to scaling and translation, the same as
\|(A_{i}-A_{j})^{\top}\widehat{U}\|^{2}
. Notably, it is shown to be adaptive to the rank of
\kappa
. Per
Corollary

4
, if
\mathrm{rk}(\kappa)=\infty
, then
T(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})
converges in distribution to
\mathcal{N}(0,2)
. In contrast, if
\mathrm{rk}(\kappa)=r^{*}<\infty
, then the limiting distribution of
T(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})
depends on the eigenvalues of the
r^{*}\times r^{*}
matrix
U^{\top}\mathcal{D}^{2}U
and is thus less elegant, seeing as we need to additionally estimate these unknown eigenvalues unlike the setting with
\mathrm{rk}(\kappa)=\infty
. Nevertheless, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the rejection region
\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}=\{T(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})>c^{*}_{1-\alpha}\}
yields a test procedure with asymptotic significance level
\alpha
, where
c^{*}_{1-\alpha}
is the
1-\alpha)
quantile of

\frac{1}{\|\widehat{U}^{\top}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{2}\widehat{U}\|_{F}}\sum_{s=1}^{r}(Z_{s}^{2}-1)\lambda_{s}(\widehat{U}^{\top}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{2}\widehat{U}).

(4.16)

We emphasize that
c^{*}_{1-\alpha}
is a function depending only on the
estimate

\widehat{U}^{\top}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{2}\widehat{U}
and can be obtained by parametric bootstrap or by using the algorithm in
[
28
]
.
∎

Remark 14

(Comparisons to other potential test statistics)
.

One might ask whether there are test statistics similar to
T
in
Corollary

4
but that are simpler to compute or admit simpler limiting distributions. To address this question, we consider the following four secondary test statistics and show that all of them are inferior to
T
, either practically or theoretically.

Specifically, define

1.

T_{1}(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})=\|A_{i}-A_{j}\|^{2}
,

2.

T_{2}(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})=\|(A_{i}-A_{j})^{\top}\widehat{D}^{-1}\widehat{U}\|^{2}
,

3.

T_{3}(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})=\|\widehat{X}_{i}-\widehat{X}_{j}\|^{2}=\|(A_{i}-A_{j})^{\top}\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-1/2}\|^{2}
,

4.

T_{4}(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})=(\widehat{X}_{i}-\widehat{X}_{j})^{\top}(\widehat{\Sigma}(\widehat{X}_{i})+\widehat{\Sigma}(\widehat{X}_{j}))^{-1}(\widehat{X}_{i}-\widehat{X}_{j})
.

Above,
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
, and
T_{3}
are variants of the
\ell_{2}
norm distance between rows
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
, where
\widehat{D}
is a diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are some estimates of
d_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq n
, whereas
T_{4}
is the Mahalanobis distance between the (truncated) embeddings
\widehat{X}_{i}
and
\widehat{X}_{j}
.

For
T_{1}
, we have
T_{1}(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})=\sum_{k}Y_{k}
, where
Y_{k}
are independent Bernoulli random variables with success probabilities
p_{ik}(1-p_{jk})+p_{jk}(1-p_{ik})
. Now, consider
p_{ik}=1/2
, so
p_{ik}(1-p_{jk})+p_{jk}(1-p_{ik})=1/2
, which does not depend on the value of
p_{jk}
. Therefore, if
p_{ik}\equiv 1/2
for all
k
then the distribution of
T_{1}(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})
does not depend on
\{p_{jk}\}
and we cannot use
T_{1}
to construct a consistent test procedure.

For
T_{2}
, by using the same arguments as for
Theorem

4
, we have that

r^{-1/2}(\|(A_{i}-A_{j})\mathcal{D}^{-1}U\|^{2}-r)\rightsquigarrow r^{-1/2}(\chi^{2}_{r}-r)

as
n\to\infty
under the null hypothesis. We might therefore hope that
r^{-1/2}(T_{2}(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})-r)
also converges to
r^{-1/2}(\chi^{2}_{r}-r)
, thereby yielding a simpler limiting distribution that that of
T
. However, the main difficulty with using
T_{2}
is in defining an estimate
\widehat{D}
. Indeed, a natural choice for the entries of
\widehat{D}
is either the plug-in estimates
\widehat{d}_{k}=(\widehat{p}_{ik}(1-\widehat{p}_{ik})+\widehat{p}_{jk}(1-\widehat{p}_{jk}))^{1/2}
, where
\widehat{p}_{k\ell}
are the entries of
\widehat{U}\widehat{\Lambda}\widehat{U}^{\top}
, or
\widehat{d}_{k}=a_{ik}-a_{jk}
. The first choice is problematic in that, as
Remark

10
shows,
\widehat{p}_{k\ell}
might converge arbitrarily slowly to
p_{k\ell}
. The second choice is also problematic as
a_{ik}-a_{jk}=0
with non-zero probability and so
\widehat{D}^{-1}
may be undefined. In contrast,
T
only depends on the Frobenius norm of
\widehat{U}^{\top}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{2}\widehat{U}
and is thus robust to the specific values of a large number of entries of
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
.

For
T_{3}
, write
M_{3}=\mathcal{D}U\Lambda^{-1}U^{\top}\mathcal{D}
. Once again, using the same arguments as for
Theorem

4
, we have that

\frac{T_{3}(\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})-\mathrm{tr}\,M_{3}}{\|M_{3}\|_{F}}\rightsquigarrow\frac{1}{\|M_{3}\|_{F}}\sum_{s=1}^{r}(Z_{s}^{2}-1)\lambda_{s}(M_{3})

as
n\to\infty
under the null hypothesis. The limiting distributions of
T
and
T_{3}
are therefore quite similar if
r
is fixed as
n\rightarrow\infty
but can be qualitatively different when
r\rightarrow\infty
with
n
. Recall that if
r\rightarrow\infty
, then
\|M^{*}\|_{F}^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{r}(Z_{s}^{2}-1)\lambda_{s}(M^{*})\rightsquigarrow N(0,2)
as
n\to\infty
under the null hypothesis, which is a consequence of the Lindeberg–Feller central limit theorem. The non-zero eigenvalues of
M^{*}
are all of order
\rho_{n}
and thus
\max_{s\leq r}\|M^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\lambda_{s}(M^{*})^{2}\asymp r^{-1}\rightarrow 0
. In contrast, if
s\leq r
, then
|\lambda_{s}(M_{3})|\asymp\rho_{n}|\lambda_{s}^{-1}|
and thus

\max_{s\leq r}\frac{\lambda_{s}(M_{3})^{2}}{\|M_{3}\|_{F}^{2}}\asymp\frac{\lambda_{r}^{-2}}{\sum_{s=1}^{r}\lambda_{s}^{-2}}\asymp\frac{\mu_{r}^{-2}}{\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mu_{s}^{-2}},

where as before
\{\mu_{s}\}_{s\geq 1}
are the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
. Now, if
\{\mu_{s}\}_{s\geq 1}
decays to zero sufficiently rapidly, then
\max_{s\leq r}\lambda_{s}(M_{3})^{2}/\|M_{3}\|_{F}^{2}
does not converge to zero. Concretely, for
\mu_{s}\asymp e^{-s}
, we have
e^{2r}/(\sum_{s=1}^{r}e^{2s})\succsim 1
. Thus, we cannot apply the Lindeberg–Feller CLT to guarantee that
T_{3}
, properly scaled and translated, converges to a normal distribution.

The distribution of
T_{4}
has been previously studied in
[
25
,
27
]
when
\kappa
is a finite rank kernel. There, it has been shown that
T_{4}
converges to a central (non-central, resp.)
\chi^{2}_{r}
under the null (local alternative, resp.) hypothesis. If instead
\kappa
is an infinite rank kernel and
r
is fixed then, by combining the normal approximation in
Corollary

2
with the analysis in
[
25
]
, we can show that
T_{4}
